# Earlier conversation the compactor did NOT receive (before the previous summary)
[0] user: "Druid's window function engine supports only positional row offsets (`WindowFrame.Rows`) and peer-group offsets (`WindowFrame.Groups`). Support native value-based `RANGE` window framing across the query engine, on-heap aggregation, value processors, and SQL planner/validator.

Implement a new native window frame type `WindowFrame.Range` as a public nested class in `WindowFrame`, registered as Jackson subtype `\"range\"`. It accepts `@Nullable Double lowerOffset`, `@Nullable Double upperOffset`, and a non-null `ColumnWithDirection orderBy`, exposed via `getLowerOffset()`, `getUpperOffset()`, and `getOrderBy()`. A null offset represents a positionally unbounded limit (`UNBOUNDED`), while a non-null offset is a signed distance in the ordering key's value space. Provide a static factory method `WindowFrame.range(@Nullable Double lowerOffset, @Nullable Double upperOffset, ColumnWithDirection orderBy)`. `WindowFrame.Range` is a standalone `WindowFrame` implementation (not extending `OffsetFrame`), overrides `equals` and `hashCode` by value, and supports Jackson serialization and deserialization.

Extend framed aggregation (`FramedOnHeapAggregatable#aggregateAll`) and value processors (`WindowFramedAggregateProcessor`, `WindowFirstProcessor`, `WindowLastProcessor`) to accept `WindowFrame.Range`. Frame resolution over sorted partitions must observe:
- For a non-null ordering key value `v` and finite offset `o`, the bound value is `v + o` when ordered ascending (`ASC`) and `v - o` when ordered descending (`DESC`), forming a closed interval `[lowerBound, upperBound]`.
- Unbounded bounds (`UNBOUNDED PRECEDING` / `UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING`) remain positional, extending to the partition boundaries and including rows with `NULL` ordering keys.
- When the current row's ordering key is `NULL`, finite-offset bounds resolve to the boundaries of the contiguous run of `NULL` ordering values containing that row (its peer group).
- Rows with equal ordering key values (peers) resolve to identical frame boundaries and receive identical output values.
- Empty frames emit the aggregator's zero-row default value, or `null` for `FIRST_VALUE` and `LAST_VALUE`.
- A `Range` frame covering the full partition for every row evaluates identically to `WindowFrame.unbounded()`.
- Resolution must support numeric ordering columns (`LONG`, `FLOAT`, `DOUBLE`, where timestamp columns reach the native engine as `LONG`) across all `RowsAndColumns` implementations. Ordering column types that do not support value arithmetic with finite offsets must throw an unchecked `DruidException`.

Update the SQL validator and Calcite window planning to accept offset-based `RANGE` window frames (`RANGE BETWEEN <offset> PRECEDING AND <offset> FOLLOWING`, including one-sided and `CURRENT ROW` variations) when the window has exactly one `ORDER BY` key. The ordering key may be numeric or a timestamp column; the bound literal may be a numeric magnitude or a day-time `INTERVAL`. Day-time `INTERVAL` offsets must map to their millisecond magnitude as a floating-point offset without integer truncation. Unsupported `RANGE` window forms (such as multiple `ORDER BY` keys or non-numeric/non-interval ordering keys with finite bounds) must fail validation or planning.
"

28: /**
29:  * A semantic interface used to aggregate a list of AggregatorFactories across a given set of data
30:  * <p>
31:  * The aggregation specifically happens on-heap and should be used in places where it is known that the data
32:  * set can be worked with entirely on-heap.  There is support for frame definitions, frames aggregate certain
33:  * subsets of rows in a rolling fashion like a windowed average.  Frames are defined in terms of boundaries
34:  * where a boundary could be based on rows or it could be based on "PEER" groupings.
35:  * <p>
36:  * A peer grouping is defined as a set of rows that are the same based on the ORDER BY columns specified.  As such

27: /**
28:  * A semantic interface used to partition a data set based on a given set of columns.
29:  * <p>
30:  * This specifically assumes that it is working with pre-clustered data and, as such, the groups returned
31:  * should be contiguous and unique (that is, all rows for a given combination of values exist in only one grouping)
32:  */
33: public interface ClusteredGroupPartitioner
34: {
35:   static ClusteredGroupPartitioner fromRAC(RowsAndColumns rac)
36:   {
37:     ClusteredGroupPartitioner retVal = rac.as(ClusteredGroupPartitioner.class);
38:     if (retVal == null) {
39:       retVal = new DefaultClusteredGroupPartitioner(rac);
40:     }
41:     return retVal;
42:   }
43:

45:    * Computes and returns a list of contiguous boundaries for independent

Now I have a good understanding of the SQL side. Let me look at the WindowGroup's `getWindowFrame` and `getBoundAsInteger` methods - this is where SQL planning converts RexWindowBound to native WindowFrame. Now let me understand the whole flow:

1. SQL query comes with a window frame (e.g., `RANGE BETWEEN 3 PRECEDING AND 2 FOLLOWING`)
2. `DruidSqlValidator.validateWindow` validates the frame - currently rejects offset-based RANGE
3. `Windowing.WindowGroup.getWindowFrame()` converts the Calcite RexWindowBound to a native WindowFrame

[27] assistant: [reasoning]
Now let me understand the Windowing.WindowGroup's use of `getConstant`. Note that window frame offsets in the Calcite plan appear as `RexInputRef` into the window's `constants` list. 

For a RANGE frame with a numeric literal offset e.g. `RANGE BETWEEN 3 PRECEDING AND 2 FOLLOWING`, the bound would be a RexInputRef that points to a constant with a numeric value (BigDecimal). For day-time intervals like `RANGE BETWEEN INTERVAL '1' DAY PRECEDING`, the constant would be a long value of millis (e.g., 86400000).

Let me check how `getBoundAsInteger` works: it casts `bound.getOffset()` to `RexInputRef` and gets the constant from `window.constants`. The constant's value is a `Number` and `intValue()` is taken.

For RANGE frames, the offset value can be a Double (floating point). For intervals, the value would be the number of milliseconds as a Long. We want to avoid integer truncation of day-time intervals: "Day-time `INTERVAL` offsets must map to their millisecond magnitude as a floating-point offset without integer truncation."

So for `getBoundAsDouble`, we'd get the constant's value as `Number` and call `doubleValue()`.

Now let me check how Calcite represents the frame offset constants. For a window like `OVER (ORDER BY x RANGE BETWEEN 3 PRECEDING AND 2 FOLLOWING)`, the `Window.rel` will have constants list containing RexLiteral for 3 and 2. The `RexWindowBound.getOffset()` returns a RexNode (RexInputRef pointing into constants). Let me check how the value can be retrieved: `window.constants.get(refIndex - sig.size()).getValue()`.

For a numeric literal like `3`, `RexLiteral.getValue()` returns a BigDecimal (for exact numeric) or Integer/Long. For interval day-time, the value is a `BigDecimal` of milliseconds? Let me check. In Calcite, `SqlIntervalLiteral.IntervalValue` has value as milliseconds... Actually in RexLiteral, an interval day-time type has value as `BigDecimal` (millis). Let me verify: `RexLiteral.intValue()` for intervals returns the millis value. Hmm.

Let me check `SqlLiteral.getValue` for interval literals. In Calcite `SqlIntervalLiteral.IntervalValue` stores `intervalInMillis` as a long (BigDecimal?). Let me look at how Druid handles intervals in expressions... Let me search for existing handling of interval literals in the Druid codebase.

[37] assistant: [reasoning]
The quidem tests with `RANGE` all expect errors (multi-key). These tests will continue to expect errors after my change, which is fine (multi ORDER BY keys -> unsupported).

Wait, but "0 FOLLOWING" — with `RANGE BETWEEN 0 FOLLOWING AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING` — the window has NO order by... these would remain errors.

Hmm, actually let me think about which of these quidem tests should change behavior. Looking at the tests:
- `RANGE 0 FOLLOWING` (no BETWEEN, single bound = 0 FOLLOWING): "RANGE 0 FOLLOWING" means lower bound = current row... wait no. In SQL, `OVER (ORDER BY x RANGE 0 FOLLOWING)` means `RANGE BETWEEN 0 FOLLOWING AND CURRENT ROW`? No wait: "RANGE 0 FOLLOWING" as a single bound defines... Let me think. In SQL, `OVER (ORDER BY x RANGE <offset> FOLLOWING)` means the frame starts at `<offset> FOLLOWING` and ends at `CURRENT ROW`. Actually no.

Per SQL standard: `RANGE BETWEEN 0 FOLLOWING AND CURRENT ROW`? Hmm, in the `updateBoundsIfNeeded` method above:

```java
if (lowerBound != null && upperBound == null) {
  if (lowerBound.getKind() == SqlKind.FOLLOWING || SqlWindow.isUnboundedFollowing(lowerBound)) {
    upperBound = lowerBound;
    lowerBound = SqlWindow.createCurrentRow(SqlParserPos.ZERO);
  } else {
    upperBound = SqlWindow.createCurrentRow(SqlParserPos.ZERO);
  }
  window.setLowerBound(lowerBound);
  window.setUpperBound(upperBound);
}
```

So `RANGE 0 FOLLOWING` becomes `RANGE BETWEEN CURRENT ROW AND 0 FOLLOWING`. That means frame = [current row's value, current row's value + 0] = the peer group of the current row (all rows with equal value). This is like `WindowFrame.groups(0, 0, orderBy)` essentially, when the offset is 0.

OK. These quidem tests were recorded with errors since the current validator rejects any offset-based RANGE. After my change, single-ORDER-BY-key RANGE frames will be supported, so some of these quidem tests might produce results instead of errors! But wait — the quidem qaWin tests are pre-recorded `.iq` files; they're checked in. If my change makes previously-error queries succeed, those tests would fail (since expected is `!error`).

Hmm wait, let me re-read: "QueryInterruptedException\n!error" — the `!error` marker means quidem expects the query to fail with an error matching. Actually in quidem, `!error` expects a specific error message on the next line. "QueryInterruptedException" is the expected error text.

So if my changes make those queries succeed, the quidem tests would break. But those queries have multiple ORDER BY keys, e.g. `ORDER BY server_ip ASC, timezone, time_floor(__time, 'PT12H') RANGE 0 FOLLOWING`. Per the spec: "Unsupported RANGE window forms (such as multiple ORDER BY keys ...) must fail validation or planning." So they will still error. 

But the error message might change! The `!error` assertion in quidem checks that the error message contains/matches the given text ("QueryInterruptedException" here). Hmm, actually how does quidem's `!error` work? It asserts the message starts with or equals the text after `!error`. If the validator error changes from "Order By with RANGE clause currently supports only UNBOUNDED or CURRENT ROW..." to something else, quidem might still accept it if it only checks for "QueryInterruptedException" — hmm, that's the generic error class in the mysql output format.

## Design

### 1. `WindowFrame.Range`

```java
@JsonSubTypes(value = {
    @JsonSubTypes.Type(name = "rows", value = WindowFrame.Rows.class),
    @JsonSubTypes.Type(name = "groups", value = WindowFrame.Groups.class),
    @JsonSubTypes.Type(name = "range", value = WindowFrame.Range.class),
})
```

```java
class Range implements WindowFrame
{
  @Nullable private final Double lowerOffset;
  @Nullable private final Double upperOffset;
  private final ColumnWithDirection orderBy;

  @JsonCreator
  public Range(
      @JsonProperty("lowerOffset") @Nullable Double lowerOffset,
      @JsonProperty("upperOffset") @Nullable Double upperOffset,
      @JsonProperty("orderBy") ColumnWithDirection orderBy)
  {
    ...
  }

  getLowerOffset(), getUpperOffset(), getOrderBy()

  equals/hashCode by value
  toString
}
```

And a static factory:

```java
static Range range(@Nullable Double lowerOffset, @Nullable Double upperOffset, ColumnWithDirection orderBy)
```

Note: `WindowFrame.Range` is a standalone `WindowFrame` implementation (not extending `OffsetFrame`).

### 2. Frame resolution semantics

For each row `i` in a sorted partition, we resolve `[lowerBound, upperBound]`:
- lower offset `lo` (null = unbounded => 0), upper offset `uo` (null = unbounded => numRows).
- If current row's ordering key value `v` is non-null and offsets finite:
  - bound value lower = `v + lo` if ASC else `v - lo`... 

Wait, careful: the spec says:
"For a non-null ordering key value `v` and finite offset `o`, the bound value is `v + o` when ordered ascending (`ASC`) and `v - o` when ordered descending (`DESC`), forming a closed interval `[lowerBound, upperBound]`."

Hmm. Let me think about what this means. In standard SQL RANGE frames:
- `RANGE BETWEEN o PRECEDING AND p FOLLOWING` with ORDER BY k ASC:
  - lower bound value = v - o, upper bound value = v + p.
- With ORDER BY k DESC:
  - lower bound value = v + p, upper bound value = v - o... 

Hmm wait. In DESC ordering, "preceding" means larger values. The frame for ORDER BY k DESC RANGE BETWEEN o PRECEDING AND p FOLLOWING is [v + o, v - p] in value space... no wait:

With DESC ordering, rows are ordered from largest to smallest. "Preceding" rows are rows that come before the current row, i.e., with *larger* values. So `o PRECEDING` in DESC = `v + o` (value larger than v). "Following" = smaller values: `p FOLLOWING` in DESC = `v - p`.

So frame = values in [v + o_PRECEDING ... v - p_FOLLOWING]? Hmm, wait for DESC:
- lower bound (start of frame in row order): rows before current row have larger values. `o PRECEDING` means the frame starts at rows with value >= v + o? no...

Let me think again. ORDER BY k DESC: rows sorted descending by value. Current row at value v. Rows preceding (earlier in sort order) have values >= v (well, >= v in value terms since DESC). RANGE o PRECEDING: frame starts (in row order) at the first row whose value is within o of v, i.e. value >= v + o (for numeric where "within o" means |value - v| <= o). Rows following have values <= v. RANGE p FOLLOWING: frame ends at last row with value >= v - p, i.e. value in [v - p, v + o].

So in both ASC and DESC cases, the frame in *value* space is [v - maxOffsetPreceding, v + maxOffsetFollowing] where offsets are magnitudes... Wait:

For ASC: `o PRECEDING` => lower bound value = v - o. `p FOLLOWING` => upper bound value = v + p. Frame = [v - o, v + p].
For DESC: `o PRECEDING` => frame starts at value >= v + o... so frame = [v + o... v - p]?? That can't be right since the lower bound in value space would be v - p and upper v + o. Frame = [v - p, v + o].

Hmm wait, that's the same value interval! For DESC: frame covers values x with |x - v| <= max(o, p)... no. Hmm, let me redo.

Actually for DESC with `o PRECEDING AND p FOLLOWING`: the frame includes rows whose value x satisfies: x >= v + o isn't right either. Let's think of it as: rows in the frame are those between (in sort order) the "o PRECEDING" bound and the "p FOLLOWING" bound.

- The `o PRECEDING` bound in value terms: `v + o` (for DESC, since preceding rows have larger values, offset o "backwards" in value space means +o).
- The `p FOLLOWING` bound in value terms: `v - p` (following rows have smaller values).

The frame = all rows with value x such that x is between v - p and v + o inclusive... but specifically x >= (v - p) and x <= (v + o)?? Wait no — the frame is rows between the two bounds in *sort order*. In sort order (DESC), rows with value v + o come before rows with value v, which come before rows with value v - p. So the frame = rows with value in [v - p, v + o] (in value space). Yes! 

So for DESC `o PRECEDING AND p FOLLOWING`, value interval = [v - p, v + o].
For ASC `o PRECEDING AND p FOLLOWING`, value interval = [v - o, v + p].

Now the spec says: "the bound value is `v + o` when ordered ascending (`ASC`) and `v - o` when ordered descending (`DESC`)".

So the offset sign convention here: the spec defines lowerOffset and upperOffset as *signed distances*. E.g., `RANGE BETWEEN 3 PRECEDING AND 2 FOLLOWING` would be lowerOffset = -3, upperOffset = +2 (like Rows/Groups frames use signed offsets where PRECEDING is negative and FOLLOWING positive).

Hmm wait, but then for ASC: bound value = v + o. With lowerOffset = -3: lowerBound = v - 3. upperOffset = +2: upperBound = v + 2. That matches ASC interval [v - 3, v + 2]. 

For DESC: bound value = v - o. With lowerOffset = -3: lowerBound = v - (-3) = v + 3. upperOffset = +2: upperBound = v - 2 = v - 2. So interval = [v + 3, v - 2]?? That's backwards... Hmm.

Wait, I think I need to reconsider. Maybe the offsets in `WindowFrame.Range` are magnitudes rather than signed? "A null offset represents a positionally unbounded limit (UNBOUNDED), while a non-null offset is a signed distance in the ordering key's value space."

Hmm, "signed distance in the ordering key's value space". So lowerOffset = -3 means 3 PRECEDING, upperOffset = 2 means 2 FOLLOWING. And "the bound value is v + o when ordered ASC and v - o when ordered DESC"...

For DESC with lowerOffset = -3 (3 PRECEDING): lowerBound = v - (-3) = v + 3. And upperOffset = +2 (2 FOLLOWING): upperBound = v - 2.

So interval [v + 3, v - 2]... but wait — in DESC order, the "lower" bound (frame start) is the *higher* value. So the interval in value space is [v - 2, v + 3] as a closed interval — the frame start value (v+3) is larger than the frame end value (v-2). The interval `[lowerBound, upperBound]` notation assumes lowerBound <= upperBound which holds for ASC.

Hmm, so maybe the interpretation is: in DESC, lowerOffset = -3 gives lowerBound = v + 3 — the frame *starts* (in row order) at rows with value <= v + 3... and the frame *ends* at rows with value >= v - 2... wait no.

Ugh, let me think about which rows are IN the frame for DESC, lowerOffset = -3 (3 PRECEDING), upperOffset = 2 (2 FOLLOWING):
- Frame start (in row order): first row (scanning from partition start) whose value is "within 3 preceding" — rows before current row have larger values; 3 PRECEDING means the frame starts at rows with value <= v + 3 (i.e., value >= v + 3 would be *more* than 3 preceding... hmm).

Let me think of it differently using the ASC case and symmetry. For ASC, 3 PRECEDING: frame start value = v - 3, frame = rows with value in [v - 3, ...]. Rows with value < v - 3 are more than 3 "before" and excluded.

For DESC, 3 PRECEDING: rows before current have larger values. "3 preceding" = the frame starts at the row that is 3 units before in value space = value v + 3 (moving backwards in value space from v, since backwards in sort order = +direction). So frame start (row order) = first row with value <= v + 3. I.e., frame includes rows with value <= v + 3 (and >= ...). And 2 FOLLOWING: frame ends at last row with value >= v - 2. So frame = rows with value in [v - 2, v + 3].

So for DESC with signed offsets (lowerOffset=-3, upperOffset=+2): 
- lowerBound (frame start, exclusive boundary computed as first row >= bound): bound = v - lowerOffset = v + 3. Frame start = first row with value <= v + 3 → in terms of a search: first index i such that value[i] <= v + 3.

Hmm, so with DESC, the frame start uses `<=` on the computed bound, and the frame end = last row with value >= v - 2 → last index i such that value[i] >= v - 2.

OK here's a cleaner way to think: For a sorted partition (sorted by orderBy direction), the frame boundaries in value space:

ASC:
- lowerBoundValue = v + lowerOffset (lowerOffset <= 0 typically for PRECEDING, but could be positive for "X FOLLOWING AND Y FOLLOWING" forms... e.g. RANGE BETWEEN 2 FOLLOWING AND 4 FOLLOWING has lowerOffset = +2, upperOffset = +4).
- upperBoundValue = v + upperOffset.
- Frame = rows with value in [lowerBoundValue, upperBoundValue] (closed interval).
- Frame start row = first row with value >= lowerBoundValue.
- Frame end row = last row with value <= upperBoundValue.

DESC:
- lowerBoundValue = v - lowerOffset.
- upperBoundValue = v - upperOffset.
- Since descending, lowerBoundValue >= upperBoundValue when lowerOffset <= upperOffset... wait lowerOffset=-3: lowerBoundValue = v + 3; upperOffset = +2: upperBoundValue = v - 2. So lowerBoundValue > upperBoundValue.
- Frame = rows with value in [upperBoundValue, lowerBoundValue] i.e. [v - 2, v + 3].
- Frame start row (in row order, descending values) = first row with value <= lowerBoundValue = v + 3.
- Frame end row = last row with value >= upperBoundValue = v - 2.

Hmm OK. So in both cases, if we define:
- lo = signed lower offset (negative = PRECEDING, positive = FOLLOWING; null = unbounded)
- uo = signed upper offset

Then:
- For ASC: frame = rows x with (v + lo) <= x <= (v + uo).
- For DESC: frame = rows x with (v - uo) <= x <= (v - lo). (equivalently x <= v - lo AND x >= v - uo).

Wait for DESC: frame includes x <= v + 3 = v - lo AND x >= v - 2 = v - uo. Yes.

Alternatively, unify: bound values computed as: lowerBound = v + lo (ASC) or v - lo (DESC); upperBound = v + uo (ASC) or v - uo (DESC). Then frame = rows x with min(lowerBound, upperBound) <= x <= max(...) hmm no.

Actually wait. For DESC, is it always the case that lowerBound >= upperBound? lowerBound = v - lo, upperBound = v - uo. If lo <= uo (frame well-formed: "BETWEEN a PRECEDING AND b FOLLOWING" or "a FOLLOWING AND b FOLLOWING" with a <= b, or "a PRECEDING AND b PRECEDING" with a >= b)... Standard SQL requires lo <= uo in the sense that the start bound must not be after the end bound. E.g. "RANGE BETWEEN 3 PRECEDING AND 1 PRECEDING" is valid: lo = -3, uo = -1. Frame = rows in [v - 3... wait for ASC: [v - 3, v - 1]. For DESC: lo = -3 → lowerBound = v + 3, uo = -1 → upperBound = v + 1, frame = [v + 1, v + 3]. Makes sense.

"RANGE BETWEEN 2 FOLLOWING AND 1 FOLLOWING" is invalid in SQL (start after end). If lo = 2, uo = 1: ASC: [v + 2, v + 1] empty. So a frame with lowerBound > upperBound (in ASC terms) is empty. For empty frames, emit the aggregator's zero-row default or null for FIRST/LAST_VALUE.

Hmm, but can we rely on lo <= uo always? The native API takes arbitrary (lowerOffset, upperOffset) — both signed doubles. To be safe, if lowerBound > upperBound (ASC), the frame is empty.

Now, how does the SQL layer map "RANGE BETWEEN 3 PRECEDING AND 2 FOLLOWING" to signed offsets? Following the pattern of `getBoundAsInteger`: `bound.isPreceding() ? -value : value`. So lowerOffset = -3, upperOffset = +2. Good — consistent with the spec: "a non-null offset is a signed distance".

Hmm wait, actually, let me reconsider. Maybe the sign convention in the native `WindowFrame.Range` should be *magnitudes toward following* — let me look at the spec examples again:

"A null offset represents a positionally unbounded limit (UNBOUNDED), while a non-null offset is a signed distance in the ordering key's value space."

And the frame resolution rules:
"For a non-null ordering key value v and finite offset o, the bound value is v + o when ordered ascending (ASC) and v - o when ordered descending (DESC), forming a closed interval [lowerBound, upperBound]."

So: boundValue(v, o) = asc ? v + o : v - o. lowerBound = boundValue(v, lowerOffset), upperBound = boundValue(v, upperOffset).

For ASC with lowerOffset=-3, upperOffset=2: lowerBound = v-3, upperBound = v+2, interval [v-3, v+2]. ✓.
For DESC with lowerOffset=-3, upperOffset=2: lowerBound = v+3, upperBound = v-2. Interval [v+3, v-2] — which as a closed interval is empty... but the frame should be rows x in [v-2, v+3].

Hmm. That contradicts. Unless the sign convention for DESC frames flips: for DESC, SQL "3 PRECEDING" should map to lowerOffset = +3?? I.e., the signed distance is in *sort-order space*, not value space!

"signed distance in the ordering key's value space" — hmm. But if for DESC we map "3 PRECEDING" to lowerOffset = +3 (positive, because in value space "preceding" rows have larger values)... Then lowerBound = v - (+3) = v - 3?? No wait: DESC bound value = v - o = v - 3. And "2 FOLLOWING" → upperOffset = -2, upperBound = v - (-2) = v + 2. Interval [v - 3, v + 2]?? That's wrong too — for DESC 3 PRECEDING and 2 FOLLOWING should give [v - 2, v + 3].

Hmm hmm. Let me re-think.

Maybe the intended semantics: for DESC, "3 PRECEDING" maps to lowerOffset such that lowerBound = v + 3. Using bound = v - o: o = -3 gives v + 3. And "2 FOLLOWING" → upperBound = v - 2: o = +2. So signed offsets: PRECEDING = negative, FOLLOWING = positive, same as ASC. lowerBound = v + 3, upperBound = v - 2 for DESC.

Then "forming a closed interval [lowerBound, upperBound]" — for DESC this interval notation [v+3, v-2] is... the frame is rows whose value x satisfies upperBound <= x <= lowerBound. I.e., x ∈ [v - 2, v + 3]. So the "closed interval [lowerBound, upperBound]" is sloppy notation meaning [min, max] of the two bounds.

Actually hold on. Let me reconsider: maybe for DESC the lower/upper offsets get flipped in *magnitude* handling... Let me look at how the frame start/end resolve in row order:

Frame start (first row in frame, in partition row order) and frame end (last row in frame). For a well-formed frame (non-empty when containing current row), for DESC:
- Frame start row = first row with value <= lowerBound (since rows sorted descending, first row <= lowerBound).

Wait no! Frame start = first row (in row order) that belongs to the frame. Rows sorted DESC by value. Frame = values in [v-2, v+3] (for our example). The first row in the frame (in row order) is the first row with value <= v + 3 (i.e. <= lowerBound). The last row in frame = last row with value >= v - 2 (>= upperBound).

For ASC:
- Frame start = first row with value >= lowerBound (v - 3).
- Frame end = last row with value <= upperBound (v + 2).

So in both cases:
- ASC: start = first row with value >= lowerBound; end = last row with value <= upperBound.
- DESC: start = first row with value <= lowerBound; end = last row with value >= upperBound.

Interesting — symmetric. OK so now, this is all consistent with: boundValue(v, o) = asc ? v + o : v - o, where o is signed (PRECEDING negative, FOLLOWING positive), and:

- lowerBound = boundValue(v, lowerOffset), upperBound = boundValue(v, upperOffset).
- ASC: frame rows x: lowerBound <= x <= upperBound.
- DESC: frame rows x: upperBound <= x <= lowerBound.

And for the frame to be non-empty in ASC: lowerBound <= upperBound (lo <= uo). In DESC: upperBound <= lowerBound → v - uo <= v - lo → -uo <= -lo → uo >= lo. Same condition: lo <= uo. 

Now NULL handling:
- "When the current row's ordering key is NULL, finite-offset bounds resolve to the boundaries of the contiguous run of NULL ordering values containing that row (its peer group)."

So if current row's key is null and both offsets finite: frame = the contiguous NULL run containing the row. (Because in SQL semantics, NULL != NULL comparisons... in standard SQL, RANGE with NULL keys — hmm, actually in standard SQL, NULLs sort first (ASC) and the RANGE comparison v - o would be NULL → no rows match... but the spec here says: bounds resolve to the NULL peer group boundaries.)

- "Unbounded bounds (UNBOUNDED PRECEDING / UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING) remain positional, extending to the partition boundaries and including rows with NULL ordering keys."

So if lowerOffset = null (UNBOUNDED PRECEDING): frame start = 0 (partition start). If upperOffset = null: frame end = numRows-1.

What if current row's key is NULL and lower bound is unbounded but upper bound finite? Then lower = 0 (partition start), and upper = end of the NULL run (the peer group boundary). Because the upper finite bound resolves to the NULL run boundary.

Hmm — "finite-offset bounds resolve to the boundaries of the contiguous run of NULL ordering values containing that row (its peer group)". So a finite bound when key is NULL → the corresponding boundary of the NULL run:
- Finite lower offset with NULL key: frame start = start of NULL run.
- Finite upper offset with NULL key: frame end = end of NULL run.

That makes `RANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW`... wait CURRENT ROW is offset 0 — finite. Hmm! CURRENT ROW: in the spec, "including one-sided and CURRENT ROW variations". CURRENT ROW maps to offset 0.0? Or is CURRENT ROW handled specially?

Per standard SQL: RANGE BETWEEN x PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW means upperBound = v (current row's value), inclusive → includes all peers (rows with value == v). Offset 0 = "0 FOLLOWING"/"0 PRECEDING" which in value terms = v. So yes, CURRENT ROW == offset 0.0 works: bound value = v + 0 = v, closed interval → includes all rows with value == v (the peer group). 

And for ROWS-style CURRENT ROW it's also 0 (as in `getBoundAsInteger` → 0). So in the SQL layer, `getBoundAsDouble`: unbounded → null, currentRow → 0.0, preceding → -value, following → +value.

Now what about ordering columns with NULLs in the middle? The partition is sorted by the orderBy column, so NULLs form a contiguous run (NULLs sort first in ASC per Druid's sort... need to check how sorting works — the data comes pre-sorted via NaiveSortOperatorFactory; in Druid, NULLs sort first for ASC? For the native window engine, the sort is done by the sort operator; nulls first for ascending I believe).

Since the frame resolution must handle NULL keys: "the contiguous run of NULL ordering values containing that row". If the data is sorted with NULLs first (ASC), the NULL run is at the start of the partition. For DESC, NULLs last. Either way a contiguous run.

Also: "Rows with equal ordering key values (peers) resolve to identical frame boundaries and receive identical output values." — Naturally satisfied since bounds are computed from the value v.

Empty frames: "Empty frames emit the aggregator's zero-row default value, or null for FIRST_VALUE and LAST_VALUE."

Zero-row default value for aggregators: hmm. What's the "aggregator's zero-row default value"? For LongSumAggregatorFactory, aggregating zero rows gives 0. This is what `aggregateAll` does when the interval is empty — the AggIntervalCursor aggregates nothing... let's look at how the existing code handles empty frames.

In `DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable.aggregateAll`, `aggCursor.moveTo(aggInterval.inputRows)`; if inputRows is empty (a == b), then `newAggregators()` is called (since currentRows.a != newRows.a or ...) — hmm wait: moveTo with newRows = Interval(a, a) where a could equal currentRows.a... e.g. first interval empty [0,0): `currentRows.a == newRows.a (0 == 0) && currentRows.b (0) < newRows.b (0)` false; `currentRows.a (0) > newRows.a (0)` false → else branch: newAggregators(), loop over empty interval → aggregators fresh with zero rows aggregated. Then `getValue` → `aggregators[i].get()` — for a fresh aggregator with no rows aggregated, e.g. LongSumAggregatorFactory returns 0L; count returns 0. So empty frames naturally produce the zero-row default! Great — so for aggregation, an empty interval works naturally.

Hmm wait, but actually there's a subtlety: `moveTo` might reuse aggregators incorrectly: if newRows == currentRows (empty [0,0) twice in a row): currentRows.a==newRows.a && currentRows.b < newRows.b → 0 < 0 false; currentRows.a > newRows.a → false; else → newAggregators + empty loop. Fine.

But wait — there's a bug scenario: moveTo(Interval(0,0)) when currentRows = Interval(0, 5) — say frame shrinks to empty after covering rows [0,5): currentRows.a == newRows.a (0 == 0) && currentRows.b (5) < newRows.b (0)? false. currentRows.a (0) > newRows.a (0)? false. else: newAggregators, empty loop. Good.

What about moveTo(Interval(3,3)) when currentRows = Interval(0, 3)? currentRows.a (0) == newRows.a (3)? No. currentRows.a (0) > newRows.a (3)? No. else branch → newAggregators, empty. Good.

Hmm, but one more: moveTo(Interval(2,2)) when currentRows = Interval(0, 2): a: 0 == 2? no; 0 > 2? no; else → fresh. Good.

Edge: moveTo(Interval(a, b)) where currentRows = Interval(c, d) and newRows extends both sides → else branch → fresh. Good.

But there IS an existing edge case bug: moveTo(newRows) where newRows.a < currentRows.a AND newRows.b > currentRows.b — covered by else. And where newRows.a == currentRows.a && newRows.b == currentRows.b — a == a && b < b false; a > a false; else → fresh aggregation of same rows. Fine (just not incremental).

OK so empty frames are handled naturally by the aggregation cursor.

Now — the important design decision: where to put the Range frame resolution logic. The `FramedOnHeapAggregatable#aggregateAll` must accept `WindowFrame.Range`. `DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable.buildIteratorFor` currently handles Rows and Groups by building `AggInterval` iterables. For Range, I need to build a similar iterator that resolves value-based bounds.

Also `WindowValueProcessorBase.computeFirstOrLastValueFramed` needs Range support for FIRST_VALUE/LAST_VALUE.

And "across all `RowsAndColumns` implementations" — the resolution must work via `ColumnAccessor` on the ordering column (accessible from any RowsAndColumns via findColumn). The `DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable` is the fallback for any AppendableRowsAndColumns. Are there other FramedOnHeapAggregatable implementations? Let me check.

## Implementation plan

### 2. Frame resolution helper

I'll create a shared helper class that, given an `AppendableRowsAndColumns`/`RowsAndColumns`, a `WindowFrame.Range`, resolves each row's frame `[start, end)` — used by both `DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable` (to build AggIntervals) and `WindowValueProcessorBase.computeFirstOrLastValueFramed`.

Where should this live? Both `DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable` (in `query.rowsandcols.semantic`) and `WindowValueProcessorBase` (in `query.operator.window.value`) need it. `query.operator.window` already depends on `rowsandcols` (WindowFirstProcessor uses ClusteredGroupPartitioner.fromRAC). Let me put the resolution logic in a new class in the window package, e.g. `org.apache.druid.query.operator.window.WindowRangeFrameResolver` or maybe as a static helper inside... Hmm.

Actually, maybe better: put it in `org.apache.druid.query.rowsandcols.semantic` next to `ClusteredGroupPartitioner`? Or in the `window` package since it's window-specific: `WindowFrame` is in `query.operator.window`. I'll create `org.apache.druid.query.operator.window.RangeFrameResolver` hmm. Hmm, but the spec says "Frame resolution over sorted partitions" is needed by:
- `FramedOnHeapAggregatable#aggregateAll` (DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable)
- `WindowFramedAggregateProcessor` (delegates to FramedOnHeapAggregatable)
- `WindowFirstProcessor` / `WindowLastProcessor` (via WindowValueProcessorBase.computeFirstOrLastValueFramed)

The spec says "Extend framed aggregation (FramedOnHeapAggregatable#aggregateAll) and value processors ... to accept WindowFrame.Range" — so both the aggregation path and value path.

Design: A resolver class that computes per-row `[frameStart, frameEnd]` (inclusive/exclusive) intervals. I'll write a class `RangeFrameIntervals` or a static method `computeRangeIntervals(RowsAndColumns rac, WindowFrame.Range frame)` returning an int[][] of per-peer-group intervals... Actually since peers resolve identically, I can compute per-peer-group intervals: for each distinct value run [start, end), compute the frame interval. That's efficient and handles the "peers receive identical output values" requirement naturally.

Let me design the resolver:

```java
public class RangeFrameResolver // maybe name: WindowRangeFrameResolver
{
  // For a sorted partition and Range frame, compute for each row i the interval [lo, hi) of input rows in its frame.
  public static int[][] computeIntervals(RowsAndColumns rac, WindowFrame.Range frame)
}
```

Hmm, actually let me think about the exact API. `DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable.buildIteratorFor` returns `Iterable<AggInterval>` where AggInterval has outputRows interval and inputRows interval. For rows/groups frames, output rows = current group; for range frames, output rows = peer group (rows with equal value), input rows = resolved frame interval.

So the resolver could produce, for each peer group g (contiguous run of equal ordering values), the frame interval [frameStart, frameEnd):

- If value of peer group is NULL:
  - If lowerOffset == null (unbounded): frameStart = 0.
  - Else (finite): frameStart = start of NULL run.
  - If upperOffset == null: frameEnd = numRows.
  - Else: frameEnd = end of NULL run.
- Else v = value:
  - If lowerOffset == null: frameStart = 0. Wait — hmm. Unbounded bounds "remain positional, extending to the partition boundaries and including rows with NULL ordering keys" → frameStart = 0, frameEnd = numRows.
  - Else: lowerBound = asc ? v + lo : v - lo.
    - ASC: frameStart = first row index with value >= lowerBound. But NULL values — where are they? In a sorted partition ASC, NULLs sort first (Druid convention: nulls first on ascending). So NULL rows are at the start (for ASC). "Unbounded bounds ... including rows with NULL ordering keys" — for unbounded. For finite bounds, do NULL values fall within [lowerBound, upperBound]? In SQL semantics, NULL is not comparable, so a finite bound excludes NULLs... Actually in standard SQL, NULL ordering keys with RANGE frames: the comparison "x BETWEEN v-o AND v+o" with x NULL is NULL → row excluded. So finite-bound frames exclude NULL rows. And the current-row-is-NULL case is handled via the peer-group rule.
    
    So for ASC with finite lower bound: frameStart = first row with non-null value >= lowerBound. Since NULLs are first in ASC sort, this is: frameStart = max(firstNonNullRow, first row with value >= lowerBound). Using binary search: the values from firstNonNull..numRows are sorted ascending; find lower bound index via binary search on the sorted numeric values.
    
  - ASC: frameEnd = last row with value <= upperBound + 1 (exclusive) = upper bound index of upperBound.
  - DESC: values sorted descending; NULLs sort... in Druid DESC sort, NULLs come last (nulls first ascending = nulls last descending... let me verify with the sort implementation). For DESC: frameStart = first row with value <= lowerBound; frameEnd = last row with value >= upperBound (exclusive +1).
  
Since values can be LONG, FLOAT, DOUBLE — numeric. I can extract them as doubles... hmm, careful with precision. LONG timestamps up to ~1.7e18 (millis ~1.7e12, micros ~1.7e15, nanos ~1.7e18). Doubles have 53 bits mantissa (~9e15 exact integers). Nanosecond timestamps (1.7e18) would lose precision as doubles. But offsets are Double anyway, so bound computation happens in double space regardless. Comparisons between the bound (double) and values (long) — for exact matching semantics: e.g. `RANGE BETWEEN 86400000 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW` on __time (millis as LONG): bound = v - 86400000.0. Comparing long values x >= bound: if I convert x to double, precision loss for large x. Alternatively compare via long when both offsets and values are integral... 

To be safe, I'll do comparisons with a hybrid: extract values as double for float/double columns; for long columns, I can still compare exactly by checking... Actually, let me simplify: compare using doubles but handle precision by... Hmm.

Actually, let me think about how precise this needs to be. The offsets are Doubles. For a LONG ordering column, values could be big. v + lo in double space: if v = 2^62 and lo = 1.0, v + 1.0 can't be represented → bound = v (rounded). Then comparison x >= bound where x converted to double also loses precision. This could cause off-by-one row boundary errors for enormous longs. In practice, timestamps in millis (1.7e12) and typical numeric values are well within double precision (2^53 ≈ 9e15). Microsecond timestamps (1.7e15) are still < 9e15 — OK. Nanosecond (1.7e18) > 9e15 — precision loss possible but the offsets would also be huge; edge behavior slightly fuzzy. I'll accept double-space comparisons for LONG columns too — simplest and consistent. Hmm, but wait — exactness matters for the equality case: e.g. `RANGE BETWEEN 5 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW` on longs: bound = v - 5. x >= v - 5.0. Converting x (long) to double: for x near v (within double precision range) exact. Values beyond 2^53 would be problematic but rare.

Hmm, actually, let me do a smarter comparison for LONG columns: I can compare `long x` vs `double bound` exactly using doubleToLong / longToDouble comparisons... There's a known technique: if |bound| < 2^53, I can convert bound to long via ceil/floor and compare as longs. Actually the standard trick:

To compare long x with double b:
- if b is NaN → undefined (shouldn't happen).
- if b >= 9.223372036854776E18 (2^63) → x < b always.
- if b <= -2^63 → x >= b always (x >= Long.MIN).
- else convert b to long: bl = (long) b — but if b has fractional part, (long) b truncates toward zero. Then x >= b ⟺ x >= ceil(b) ⟺ x >= (long)Math.ceil(b). And x <= b ⟺ x <= floor(b) = (long) b (for b >= 0)... Math.floor(b) for negative also truncates down. So:
  - x >= b ⟺ x >= Math.ceil(b) [as long]
  - x <= b ⟺ x <= Math.floor(b) [as long]

This gives exact comparisons for long columns. But it adds complexity. Hmm. For the initial implementation, I could simply compare in double space via `accessor.getDouble(rowNum)`. Given the test data and typical use (timestamps in millis/micros, numeric columns), double comparison is fine and much simpler. But "Resolution must support numeric ordering columns (LONG, FLOAT, DOUBLE, where timestamp columns reach the native engine as LONG)" — the requirement is support, not exact precision at 2^63. Millis timestamps are exact in double. I'll use double comparisons via getDouble() on the accessor, which handles all three types uniformly (getDouble on Float returns (double) float value; on Long returns (double) long).

Hmm wait, one concern: FLOAT columns — getDouble(rowNum) returns the float widened to double. Offsets are doubles. Bound = v + o computed in double; comparing with other float values widened to double. Since all comparisons happen consistently in double space, and the original values were floats (exactly representable as doubles), the resolution is consistent with comparing in float space... mostly. Edge: v (float) + o (double) → double bound; float values x: compare (double)x >= bound. If o was integral, fine. The semantics "value v + offset o" in the value space — for floats, the natural interpretation is double arithmetic. OK.

Actually, you know what — let me use exact long comparison for LONG columns since it's not that hard, and double for FLOAT/DOUBLE. Hmm, but it complicates the binary search implementation. Let me think about what operations I need:

For a sorted (per orderBy direction) numeric column with NULLs contiguous (NULLs first for ASC, last for DESC — need to confirm Druid sort semantics):

- firstNonNull: index of first non-null value (0 for ASC if no nulls... wait, for ASC nulls first → nulls at start; firstNonNull = count of nulls. For DESC, nulls sort... let me check Druid's sort: `DefaultNaiveSortMaker` or the null ordering. Let me check how NULLs are sorted in the native window engine's sort.

Let me look at the sort implementation used by NaiveSortOperatorFactory.

The user's task is to implement a native `RANGE` window frame type in Apache Druid:
1. New `WindowFrame.Range` nested class (Jackson subtype "range") with `@Nullable Double lowerOffset`, `@Nullable Double upperOffset`, non-null `ColumnWithDirection orderBy`, getters, static factory `WindowFrame.range(...)`, equals/hashCode, Jackson serde.

## Objective
- Add native value-based `RANGE` window framing to Druid in `/app`.
- The new type must be `WindowFrame.Range`.
- `WindowFrame.Range` must be a public nested class of `WindowFrame`.
- `WindowFrame.Range` must be registered as Jackson subtype `"range"`.
- Multi-key `RANGE` queries must still fail when the work is done.

## Important Details
- `WindowFrame.Range` must have a `@Nullable Double lowerOffset`.
- `WindowFrame.Range` must have a `@Nullable Double upperOffset`.
- `WindowFrame.Range` must have a non-null `ColumnWithDirection orderBy`.
- The required getter for the lower offset is `getLowerOffset()`.
- The required getter for the upper offset is `getUpperOffset()`.
- The required getter for the ordering column is `getOrderBy()`.
- A null offset must mean a positionally unbounded side.
- A non-null offset must be a signed distance in the ordering key's value space.
- The required static factory is `WindowFrame.range(@Nullable Double lowerOffset, @Nullable Double upperOffset, ColumnWithDirection orderBy)`.
- `Range` must not extend `OffsetFrame`.
- `Range` must override `equals` by value.
- `Range` must override `hashCode` by value.
- `Range` must support Jackson serialization.
- `Range` must support Jackson deserialization.
- `FramedOnHeapAggregatable#aggregateAll` must accept `Range`.
- `WindowFramedAggregateProcessor` must accept `Range`.
- `WindowFirstProcessor` must accept `Range`.
- `WindowLastProcessor` must accept `Range`.
- For a non-null ordering key value `v` and a finite offset `o`, a bound must be `v + o` under `ASC`.
- For a non-null ordering key value `v` and a finite offset `o`, a bound must be `v - o` under `DESC`.
- A `Range` frame must be the closed interval between its lower bound and its upper bound.
- Unbounded sides of a `Range` frame must reach the partition edges.
- Unbounded sides of a `Range` frame must include rows whose key is `NULL`.
- When the current row's key is `NULL`, finite bounds must resolve to the edges of the contiguous `NULL` run that holds the row.
- Peers with equal keys must get identical frame bounds.
- Peers with equal keys must get identical output.
- An empty frame must yield the aggregator's zero-row default.
- `FIRST_VALUE` must return `null` on an empty frame.
- `LAST_VALUE` must return `null` on an empty frame.
- A `Range` that covers the whole partition for every row must evaluate the same as `WindowFrame.unbounded()`.
- `LONG` ordering columns must work.
- `FLOAT` ordering columns must work.
- `DOUBLE` ordering columns must work.
- Range resolution must work for every `RowsAndColumns` implementation.
- Timestamp columns reach the native engine as `LONG`.
- An ordering type that cannot do value arithmetic with a finite offset must throw an unchecked `DruidException`.
- The SQL validator must accept offset `RANGE` frames.
- The SQL validator must accept one-sided offset `RANGE` frames.
- The SQL validator must accept offset `RANGE` frames with a `CURRENT ROW` bound.
- Calcite planning must turn offset `RANGE` frames into native frames.
- Offset `RANGE` support must apply only when the window has exactly one `ORDER BY` key.
- The single key must be numeric or a timestamp.
- The bound literal may be a number.
- The bound literal may be a day-time `INTERVAL`.
- A day-time `INTERVAL` must map to its millisecond magnitude as a floating-point offset with no integer truncation.
- An offset `RANGE` frame with finite bounds on a non-numeric and non-interval key must be rejected during validation or planning.
- `WindowFrame` maps the Jackson name `"rows"` to `WindowFrame.Rows`.
- `WindowFrame` maps the Jackson name `"groups"` to `WindowFrame.Groups`.
- `WindowFrame.unbounded()` returns `rows(null, null)`.
- `ColumnWithDirection` encodes `ASC` as `ASC(1)`, read through `getDirectionInt()`.
- `ColumnWithDirection` encodes `DESC` as `DESC(-1)`, read through `getDirectionInt()`.
- `Windowing.java` builds row frames as `WindowFrame.rows(getBoundAsInteger(group.lowerBound), getBoundAsInteger(group.upperBound))`.
- `Windowing.java` builds group frames as `WindowFrame.groups(getBoundAsInteger(group.lowerBound), getBoundAsInteger(group.upperBound), getOrderingColumNames())`.
- `Windowing.java` has no range branch yet.
- `RexWindowBound` exposes `getOffset()`.
- `RexWindowBound` exposes `isPreceding()`.
- `RexWindowBound` exposes `isFollowing()`.
- `RexWindowBound` exposes `isCurrentRow()`.
- The agent judged that frame offsets reach `Windowing` as `RexInputRef` entries into `window.constants`.
- The agent judged that the constant is read as `window.constants.get(refIndex - sig.size()).getValue()`.
- The agent judged that `DruidSqlValidator.validateWindow` currently rejects every offset `RANGE` frame.
- The agent judged that this rejection is raised as a `CalciteContextException`.
- The agent judged that `updateBoundsIfNeeded` turns the single bound `RANGE 0 FOLLOWING` into `RANGE BETWEEN CURRENT ROW AND 0 FOLLOWING`.
- The `qaWin` directory holds quidem `RANGE` cases named `orderby_range_0_following_first_last.01.all.iq` through `.15.all.iq`.
- The `qaWin` directory holds quidem `RANGE` cases named `orderby_range_0_following_sum_count.01.all.iq` through `.15.all.iq`.
- The `qaWin` directory holds quidem `RANGE` cases named `orderby_range_0_preceding_first_last.01.all.iq` through `.05.all.iq`.
- The `qaWin` `RANGE` cases the agent printed have several `ORDER BY` keys or none.
- It is unconfirmed whether every `qaWin` `RANGE` case has several `ORDER BY` keys or none.
- The `qaWin` `RANGE` cases the agent printed expect a `QueryInterruptedException` line followed by `!error`.
- Decision: the planned `RANGE` branch will read offsets through a new `getBoundAsDouble` reader that takes `doubleValue()` from the offset constant's `Number`, instead of `getBoundAsInteger`.
- The reason is that `Range` offsets are `Double` values.
- The agent judged that `getBoundAsInteger` keeps only `intValue()`.
- Decision: unsupported `RANGE` forms will keep `Order By with RANGE clause currently supports only UNBOUNDED or CURRENT ROW. Use ROWS clause instead.` as the validator rejection message.
- The reason is that the multi-key `RANGE` cases in the `qaWin` quidem files then keep failing.
- Decision: the `qaWin` files are left as recorded.

### Range frame resolution shared helper

I'll create a new class `RangeFrameResolver` in `org.apache.druid.query.operator.window` (near `WindowFrame`). Hmm, but `DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable` is in `rowsandcols.semantic` and it already imports `org.apache.druid.query.operator.window.WindowFrame`, so dependency direction is fine.

Actually — where to put the per-row interval computation? Both consumers:
- `DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable.buildIteratorFor` → needs `Iterable<AggInterval>` (outputRows = peer group interval, inputRows = frame interval). But AggInterval is package-private in `rowsandcols.semantic`... So the resolver should return something generic like `int[] boundaries` (peer group boundaries) + a way to compute frame interval per peer group, OR just produce arrays of `[start, end)` per row.

Simplest clean API: a class `WindowFrameRangeResolver` hmm. Let me think about what both consumers need:

1. `DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable`: iterate over peer groups; for each peer group, outputRows = [peerStart, peerEnd), inputRows = [frameStart, frameEnd). The `AggIntervalCursor.moveTo` benefits from incremental aggregation when moving forward monotonically. For RANGE frames, frames are monotonically non-decreasing (both bounds move forward), so intervals will advance monotonically → moveTo hits the incremental branch mostly when frameStart stays same... Actually frame [a, b) sequences: a non-decreasing, b non decreasing. moveTo(newRows): currentRows.a == newRows.a && currentRows.b < newRows.b → incremental add. Else if currentRows.a > newRows.a && currentRows.b == newRows.b → incremental prepend. Else full recompute. For RANGE frames both a and b can advance simultaneously → full recompute each time. That's fine (correctness first).

2. `WindowValueProcessorBase.computeFirstOrLastValueFramed`: for each row, results[i] = accessor.getObject(first ? frameStart : frameEnd-1), or null if empty frame. Since peers are identical, iterate peer groups.

So the shared helper: given `RowsAndColumns rac` and `WindowFrame.Range frame`, produce a list of (peerGroupStart, peerGroupEnd, frameStart, frameEnd) tuples. Both consumers can then loop.

But for aggregations the natural structure is an Iterator to avoid materializing all... it's on-heap anyway, materializing int arrays is fine (numRows sized). Actually simpler: compute per-row arrays `frameStarts[]` and `frameEnds[]` (size numRows) — since peers share identical frames, rows in the same peer group get identical entries. Then:

- Aggregation: iterate groups... but the aggregation iterator structure wants per-group intervals to only emit once per group (output rows = peer group). I can produce an `Iterable<AggInterval>` from the resolver by scanning for peer group boundaries: rows i where frameEnds[i] != frameEnds[i-1] or frameStarts differ... Actually simpler: peer group = contiguous equal ordering values; but I can just detect boundaries where frame interval changes (which is the same thing — well, almost: two distinct adjacent peer groups could theoretically resolve to the same frame interval! e.g. offsets 0 PRECEDING AND 0 FOLLOWING... no wait, that includes the peer group itself so frames differ since groups differ. Frames ARE different. But in general, e.g., ASC values [1, 6], frame BETWEEN 5 PRECEDING AND 5 PRECEDING: hmm lo=uo=-5 → [v-5, v-5]. Group1 (v=1): x=−4 → empty. Group2 (v=6): x=1 → row 0. Different. It's hard to construct identical frames for distinct adjacent values since the current row itself is in the frame only if lo <= 0 <= uo... For lo, uo both negative (e.g. BETWEEN 5 PRECEDING AND 3 PRECEDING): group1 v=1: [-4, -2] empty; group2 v=2: [-3, -1] — includes row0? row0 has value 1 — no wait frame bounds are value-based: rows x in [-3, -1]: value 1 not in → empty. group3 v=6: [1, 3] → includes value-1 rows... Output rows are the peer groups (rows 0..0 vs 1..1) but inputRows are both empty → AggInterval(outputRows=[0,1), inputRows=[0,0)) then AggInterval(outputRows=[1,2), inputRows=[0,0)). The cursor: moveTo(Interval(0,0)) twice — second time currentRows.a==newRows.a(0==0) && currentRows.b(0) < newRows.b(0) false → currentRows.a > newRows.a false → else: newAggregators + empty loop. Fine, correct (empty → zero-row default).

So merging adjacent groups with identical frames is possible but I won't bother — just iterate peer groups.

[62] assistant: [reasoning]
NULLs form one contiguous peer group (compareRows(null, null) == 0). Good — `computeBoundaries` on the single orderBy column gives me peer groups with the NULL run (if any) as a single group.

Now, the resolution algorithm per peer group g with value v (null or numeric):

```
if (isNull(g)) {   // current row's ordering key is NULL
    frameStart = (lowerOffset == null) ? 0 : groupStart[g]        // boundaries of NULL run
    frameEnd   = (upperOffset == null) ? numRows : groupEnd[g]
} else {
    lowerBound = asc ? v + lowerOffset : v - lowerOffset  (if lowerOffset != null)
    upperBound = asc ? v + upperOffset : v - upperOffset  (if upperOffset != null)
    ASC:
      frameStart = (lowerOffset == null) ? 0 : firstRowWithValueAtLeast(lowerBound)   // >= lowerBound
      frameEnd   = (upperOffset == null) ? numRows : firstRowWithValueGreaterThan(upperBound)  // exclusive: > upperBound
    DESC:
      frameStart = (lowerOffset == null) ? 0 : firstRowWithValueAtMost(lowerBound)    // <= lowerBound
      frameEnd   = (upperOffset == null) ? numRows : firstRowWithValueLessThan(upperBound)  // exclusive: < upperBound
}
```

Wait — careful with ASC: rows x in [lowerBound, upperBound] (closed interval). frameStart = first row with x >= lowerBound. frameEnd (exclusive) = last row with x <= upperBound, + 1 = first row with x > upperBound.

For DESC: rows x with upperBound <= x <= lowerBound... wait no. Earlier analysis: DESC frame = x <= lowerBound AND x >= upperBound, where lowerBound = v - lo, upperBound = v - uo. Since sorted DESC, the frame rows are contiguous: frameStart = first row (in row order) with x <= lowerBound; frameEnd = last row with x >= upperBound + 1 = first row (scanning) with x < upperBound... 

Hmm wait, but since data is sorted DESC, rows with x >= upperBound form a *suffix*... no: sorted DESC means x[0] >= x[1] >= ... Rows with x >= upperBound: those are the *later* rows (smaller values at the end). Rows with x <= lowerBound: prefix. So frame = [firstIdx(x <= lowerBound), firstIdx(x < upperBound))... let me verify: rows x sorted desc. x <= lowerBound defines a prefix (since lowerBound >= current value... hmm not necessarily, but within the frame logic: lowerBound = v - lo where lo <= 0 (PRECEDING) → lowerBound >= v → x <= lowerBound is a prefix containing current row). upperBound = v - uo where uo >= 0 → upperBound <= v → x >= upperBound is a suffix containing current row. Frame = prefix ∩ suffix = [firstIdx(x <= lowerBound), firstIdx(x < upperBound)). Yes — frameStart = first row with x <= lowerBound; frameEnd (exclusive) = first row with x < upperBound.

And NULLs in DESC sort: nulls compare as smallest → sorted DESC (multiplied by -1) → nulls at the END of partition. So for DESC with finite bounds, NULL rows (at end) are excluded automatically since x < upperBound... wait, NULL rows at end have "value" null — comparisons: null <= lowerBound? In my binary search over doubles I'd read getDouble(null row) = 0.0 — WRONG. I must handle NULLs explicitly: treat the NULL run as outside numeric comparisons. So bounds searching happens within the non-null region:

- ASC: nulls at start → non-null region = [nullRunEnd, numRows). frameStart = max(nullRunEnd, searchStart) — i.e., search for first x >= lowerBound within [nullRunEnd, numRows). frameEnd = first x > upperBound within [nullRunEnd, numRows) — but also frameEnd >= nullRunEnd (empty if upperBound < all values). Unbounded lower → frameStart = 0 (includes nulls). Unbounded upper → frameEnd = numRows.
- DESC: nulls at end → non-null region = [0, nullRunStart). frameStart = search first x <= lowerBound within [0, nullRunStart); unbounded lower → 0. frameEnd = first x < upperBound within [0, nullRunStart)... hmm wait, frameEnd must be <= nullRunStart: rows beyond nullRunStart are nulls — since x >= upperBound is a suffix of the non-null region and nulls are after... The frame can't include null rows when bounds finite: frameEnd = min(computed, nullRunStart)? Let me think: in the non-null region sorted DESC, x < upperBound defines... x sorted desc, upperBound fixed: x < upperBound is satisfied by later rows (smaller x). firstIdx(x < upperBound) — that's the boundary. Null rows are after the non-null region; null >= upperBound is undefined/false in SQL semantics — so frameEnd = firstIdx(x < upperBound) which is within [0, nullRunStart]; if ALL non-null values >= upperBound, frameEnd = nullRunStart (nulls excluded). So searching within [0, nullRunStart) and defaulting to nullRunStart handles it.

Now — the spec says "Unbounded bounds (UNBOUNDED PRECEDING / UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING) remain positional, extending to the partition boundaries and including rows with NULL ordering keys." So unbounded sides → partition edge (0 or numRows), including nulls. ✓.

And "When the current row's ordering key is NULL, finite-offset bounds resolve to the boundaries of the contiguous run of NULL ordering values containing that row (its peer group)." → for null-key rows: finite lower → groupStart (start of NULL run), finite upper → groupEnd (end of NULL run). Unbounded → partition edge. ✓.

Implementation of binary search helpers (on the sorted non-null region):

```java
// find first index in [from, to) where predicate holds (predicate monotonic)
```

ASC: values a[from..to) sorted ascending.
- firstIdxGE(bound): smallest i with val[i] >= bound → lower-bound binary search.
- firstIdxGT(bound): smallest i with val[i] > bound → same with > comparison.

DESC: values sorted descending.
- firstIdxLE(bound): smallest i with val[i] <= bound.
- firstIdxLT(bound): smallest i with val[i] < bound.

Note: firstIdxGT(bound) in ASC region [from,to): if none, returns `to`. firstIdxLE in DESC region: if none (all values > bound), returns `to` (== nullRunStart for desc → nulls excluded ✓).

So in `Windowing.getWindowFrame()`:

```java

  return WindowFrame.unbounded();
}

  return WindowFrame.rows(...);
}

  // RANGE with at least one finite offset bound
  return WindowFrame.range(getBoundAsDouble(lower), getBoundAsDouble(upper), getSingleOrderingColumn());
} else {
  return WindowFrame.groups(...);  // existing peer-based path for UNBOUNDED/CURRENT ROW
}
```

Important finding — many qaWin RANGE queries have **no ORDER BY at all** (`OVER (PARTITION BY client_ip RANGE 0 FOLLOWING)`). Standard SQL requires ORDER BY for RANGE frames — and Calcite's own validation: does `super.validateWindow` reject RANGE frames without ORDER BY? These queries currently fail with "QueryInterruptedException" recorded — the failure could be Druid's check (any RANGE non-unbound/non-current → reject) before Calcite's own checks.

Hmm wait — but if I relax Druid's validator, `OVER (PARTITION BY client_ip RANGE 0 FOLLOWING)` (no ORDER BY) would... need ORDER BY for a Range frame! WindowFrame.range requires an orderBy column. Without ORDER BY there's no ordering → can't resolve values. So "no ORDER BY" must also be rejected for offset RANGE.

So the validator rule becomes:
- RANGE with any offset bound (PRECEDING/FOLLOWING with literal) requires:
  - exactly ONE ORDER BY key
  - (type check: numeric or timestamp — at validator or planning?)

For the type check location: at validation time, getting the type of the ORDER BY expression requires type derivation. `validateWindow` is called during validation; I could check `call` — hmm, the ORDER BY item is an arbitrary SqlNode. Deriving types: SqlValidator `deriveType(scope, node)` — I DO have `scope`! But which scope is passed? `validateWindow(windowOrId, scope, call)` — scope is the scope in which the *window declaration* is validated... Actually in Calcite's SqlValidatorImpl.validateWindow calls — `validateWindow(windowOrId, scope, call)` where scope = `scopes.get(call)` hmm. For safety, checking the type could be fragile.

Alternative plan: **validator checks structural constraints only** (exactly one ORDER BY key; bound is a numeric literal or day-time interval literal — hmm, the bound literal check: `isValidEndpoint` already requires `SqlUtil.isLiteral(boundVal)` for PRECEDING/FOLLOWING. That allows ANY literal type (e.g. string literal '5 PRECEDING'!). A string literal offset must be rejected — "The bound literal may be a number" or "a day-time INTERVAL").

So validator: for offset RANGE:
- exactly one ORDER BY key (count check on orderList)
- each finite bound literal: numeric literal or day-time interval literal
- (leave ORDER BY key type check to planning, where RowSignature is available — more robust for expressions like time_floor(__time, ...))

193:    *
194:    * Equivalent to {@link #getColumnTypeForRelDataType(RelDataType)}, but ignores

199:   @Nullable

201:   {
202:     final SqlTypeName sqlTypeName = type.getSqlTypeName();
203:     if (SqlTypeName.FLOAT == sqlTypeName) {
204:       return ColumnType.FLOAT;
205:     } else if (isDoubleType(sqlTypeName)) {
206:       return ColumnType.DOUBLE;
207:     } else if (isLongType(sqlTypeName)) {
208:       return ColumnType.LONG;
209:     } else if (isStringType(sqlTypeName) || sqlTypeName == SqlTypeName.NULL) {
210:       return ColumnType.STRING;
211:     } else if (SqlTypeName.OTHER == sqlTypeName) {
212:       if (type instanceof RowSignatures.ComplexSqlType) {

[115] assistant: [reasoning]
I'm going too deep. The pragmatic approach: in `getBoundAsDouble`, handle the constant generally:

```java
private Double getBoundAsDouble(RexWindowBound bound)
{
  if (bound.isUnbounded()) return null;
  if (bound.isCurrentRow()) return 0.0d;
  final RexLiteral constant = window.constants.get(((RexInputRef) bound.getOffset()).getIndex() - sig.size());
  final Number number = (Number) constant.getValue();  // BigDecimal for both numeric and interval
  final double value = number.doubleValue();
  return bound.isPreceding() ? -value : value;
}
```

Key insight: `RexLiteral.getValue()` returns `Comparable`. Now it's clear:

- **Day-time interval** SqlLiteral → `getValueAs(BigDecimal.class)` = `BigDecimal.valueOf(getSign() * intervalToMillis(intervalValue))` — i.e. **milliseconds** (signed).
- **Year-month interval** → months (line 364 area: `getSign() * intervalToMonths` → Long → BigDecimal.valueOf — wait, let me check: the block at 364 was in the year-month case — 17-19 ordinals → the months one. It also supports BigDecimal via `Long` → `BigDecimal.valueOf(long)`.

So both interval kinds produce BigDecimal of months-or-millis. The spec says day-time INTERVAL → millis; year-month → we should reject (Druid doesn't have month arithmetic on timestamps... `INTERVAL '1' MONTH PRECEDING` — value in months; timestamps in millis — 1 month ≠ fixed millis. So year-month intervals should be rejected).

How do I distinguish in `getBoundAsDouble`? The RexLiteral's type: `constant.getType().getSqlTypeName()` is in the `SqlTypeName.INTERVAL` family: `SqlTypeName.INTERVAL_DAY`, `INTERVAL_YEAR`, etc. Or use `SqlTypeName.getFamily() == SqlTypeFamily.INTERVAL_DAY_TIME` vs `SqlTypeFamily.INTERVAL_YEAR_MONTH`. Good — the validator check: "The bound literal may be a number... or a day-time INTERVAL... Let me synthesize the conversation and prior summary into a new comprehensive summary.

Key objective: Add native value-based RANGE window framing to Druid in /app, with `WindowFrame.Range` class, SQL validator relaxation, planning support, native resolution logic, and tests.

The conversation continued the design work:
- Design of `WindowFrame.Range` with JsonSubTypes registration "range"
- Frame resolution semantics: detailed analysis of ASC/DESC bound computation: boundValue(v, o) = asc ? v + o : v - o, signed offsets (PRECEDING negative, FOLLOWING positive, CURRENT ROW 0.0, UNBOUNDED null)
- ASC frame: rows x with lowerBound <= x <= upperBound; frameStart = first row >= lowerBound, frameEnd = first row > upperBound (exclusive)
- DESC frame: frameStart = first row <= lowerBound, frameEnd = first row < upperBound
- NULL handling: nulls sort first ASC / last DESC (compareRows returns -1 for null lhs, multiplied by direction for DESC); nulls form contiguous run; when current row key is NULL, finite bounds resolve to NULL run boundaries; unbounded bounds positional to partition edges including nulls
- Empty frames: aggregator zero-row default naturally via existing AggIntervalCursor.moveTo behavior with empty intervals
- Investigated FramedOnHeapAggregatable implementations: only DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable exists; concrete RACs (QueryableIndexRowsAndColumns etc.) don't implement it via as()
- BinarySearchableAccessor exists but assumes sorted
- ColumnAccessor interface with getDouble etc.
- DefaultNaiveSortMaker uses accessor.compareRows * direction
- LongColumnAccessorBase: nulls sort first in ASC (compareRows returns -1 for null lhs), DESC flips via direction multiplier
- ClusteredGroupPartitioner.fromRAC(rac).computeBoundaries(columns) gives peer group boundaries; nulls form one contiguous peer group (compareRows(null,null)==0)
- Resolution algorithm designed with binary search on sorted non-null region, double comparisons via getDouble (precision caveat beyond 2^53 accepted)
- Resolver class design: shared helper class `RangeFrameResolver` (name tentative) in `org.apache.druid.query.operator.window` package, exposing groupBoundaries, frameStart(g), frameEnd(g)
- DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable.buildIteratorFor needs Range branch producing Iterable<AggInterval> with outputRows = peer group, inputRows = frame interval
- isEffectivelyUnbounded for Range: both offsets null → unbounded
- WindowValueProcessorBase.computeFirstOrLastValueFramed needs Range branch: value = first ? getObject(frameStart) : getObject(frameEnd-1) if frameStart < frameEnd else null
- SQL layer:
  - DruidSqlValidator.validateWindow lines ~153-160: current rejection "Order By with RANGE clause currently supports only UNBOUNDED or CURRENT ROW. Use ROWS clause instead."
  - Windowing.WindowGroup.getWindowFrame at lines 446-461: unbounded → WindowFrame.unbounded(); isRows → rows(); else groups(...); getBoundAsInteger reads bound offsets
  - isUnboundedOrCurrent, isValidEndpoint, updateBoundsIfNeeded helpers verified
  - updateBoundsIfNeeded: single bound RANGE 0 FOLLOWING → lowerBound = CURRENT ROW, upperBound = 0 FOLLOWING
  - Calcite SqlWindow.isRows() is boolean literal; ROWS=true, RANGE=false; GROUPS token exists but NOT parsed by Calcite 1.41 parser (token 496 ROWS → createBoolean(true), token 449 RANGE → createBoolean(false); only two tokens in frame switch); so !isRows() ⟺ RANGE; SQL GROUPS frames cannot be parsed
  - Calcite Window.Group Rel level has only boolean isRows — RANGE/GROUPS conflated
  - RowSignature maps TIMESTAMP SqlTypeName → ColumnType.LONG via Calcites.getColumnTypeForRelDataType / isLongType includes TIMESTAMP — so planning-time type check sig.getColumnType(fieldIndex) for LONG/FLOAT/DOUBLE covers timestamps
  - Interval literals: day-time interval SqlLiteral.getValueAs(BigDecimal.class) → BigDecimal.valueOf(sign * SqlParserUtil.intervalToMillis(intervalValue)) — millis; year-month → months (should reject year-month)
  - RexWindowBound offsets are RexInputRef into window.constants; constant.getValue() returns BigDecimal/Number; getBoundAsDouble design: unbounded→null, currentRow→0.0, preceding→-value, following→+value; ((Number) constant.getValue()).doubleValue()

Decisions made:
- Validator relaxation: allow RANGE with offset bounds only when window has exactly one ORDER BY key; keep the old rejection message otherwise; reject offset RANGE without ORDER BY (no ORDER BY → falls into rejection since orderList empty → count != 1)
- Bound literal kind check at validator: numeric literal or day-time interval literal (isValidEndpoint already requires SqlUtil.isLiteral)
- Key type check at planning time in Windowing.getWindowFrame via sig.getColumnType (LONG/FLOAT/DOUBLE; TIMESTAMP maps to LONG) — since type derivation at validation is risky; planning-time rejection for non-numeric keys (STRING etc.) — but note ROW_NUMBER doesn't call getWindowFrame, so `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY dim1 RANGE ...)` (string key) would plan fine and succeed (ROW_NUMBER ignores frame) — this changes testUnSupportedRangeBounds behavior → test must be updated
- Keep GROUPS path for unbounded/current-row forms (no offset) to preserve existing behavior incl. string keys; only emit Range when at least one bound is finite offset (PRECEDING/FOLLOWING with literal)
- Year-month intervals rejected (intervalToMonths)
- Double comparisons via accessor.getDouble(rowNum) for LONG/FLOAT/DOUBLE — accept precision caveat beyond 2^53
- Resolver shared helper class in org.apache.druid.query.operator.window package (both DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable and WindowValueProcessorBase can import)
- AggInterval with empty inputRows handled naturally by existing moveTo/AggIntervalCursor logic → zero-row defaults
- FIRST_VALUE/LAST_VALUE empty frame → null (same pattern as existing: if frameStart < frameEnd)

```java
if (!targetWindow.isRows() && (!isUnboundedOrCurrent(lowerBound) || !isUnboundedOrCurrent(upperBound))) {
  // offset RANGE frame
  if (targetWindow.getOrderList() == null || targetWindow.getOrderList().size() != 1) {
    throw buildCalciteContextException("Order By with RANGE clause currently supports only UNBOUNDED or CURRENT ROW. Use ROWS clause instead.", windowOrId);
  }
  // check bound literals are numeric or day-time interval (year-month rejected)
}
```

Also the getBoundAsDouble design:
```java
private Double getBoundAsDouble(RexWindowBound bound)
{
  if (bound.isUnbounded()) return null;
  if (bound.isCurrentRow()) return 0.0d;
  final RexLiteral constant = window.constants.get(((RexInputRef) bound.getOffset()).getIndex() - sig.size());
  final double value = ((Number) constant.getValue()).doubleValue();
  return bound.isPreceding() ? -value : value;
}
```
Year-month interval rejection via constant.getType().getSqlTypeName().getFamily() == INTERVAL_YEAR_MONTH → throw.

RangeFrameResolver design (name tentative):
- constructor(RowsAndColumns rac, WindowFrame.Range frame)
- uses ClusteredGroupPartitioner.fromRAC(rac).computeBoundaries(ImmutableList.of(frame.getOrderBy().getColumnName())) for peer groups
- accessor = rac.findColumn(orderBy column).toAccessor()
- type check: ColumnType LONG/FLOAT/DOUBLE → ok; else if finite offsets → DruidException (unchecked); both null → treat as unbounded
- binary search helpers for ASC (firstIdxGE, firstIdxGT) and DESC (firstIdxLE, firstIdxLT) on getDouble values within non-null region
- null run boundaries: ASC nulls at start, DESC nulls at end

For DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable:
- aggregateAll accepts Range → buildIteratorFor Range branch → Iterable<AggInterval> per peer group
- isEffectivelyUnbounded: Range with both offsets null → unbounded (equiv WindowFrame.unbounded())

Also from the conversation: AggIntervalCursor.moveTo analysis — empty intervals handled correctly (fresh aggregators → zero-row default).

Also from the conversation: DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable imports WindowFrame.Rows, Groups; buildUnboundedIteratorFor; GroupIteratorForWindowFrame; invertedOrderForLastK.

Also the design statement: for aggregation path, iterate groups in natural order, moveTo recomputes unless extendable — fine for correctness.

Also: "getWindowFrame" logic:
```java
public WindowFrame getWindowFrame()
{
  if (group.lowerBound.isUnbounded() && group.upperBound.isUnbounded()) {
    return WindowFrame.unbounded();
  }
  if (group.isRows) {
    return WindowFrame.rows(getBoundAsInteger(group.lowerBound), getBoundAsInteger(group.upperBound));
  }
  if (hasOffsetBound(group.lowerBound) || hasOffsetBound(group.upperBound)) {
    // RANGE with at least one finite offset
    // validate single ordering key + type via sig
    return WindowFrame.range(getBoundAsDouble(group.lowerBound), getBoundAsDouble(group.upperBound), getSingleOrderingColumn());
  }
  return WindowFrame.groups(getBoundAsInteger(group.lowerBound), getBoundAsInteger(group.upperBound), getOrderingColumNames());
}
```

Note: the check "at least one bound is PRECEDING/FOLLOWING offset" determines Range vs Groups. For both-current or unbounded+current → keep groups path (works for all types incl strings).

Also: Windowing line 98-100: WindowFirstProcessor/WindowLastProcessor take agg.getGroup().getWindowFrame() — they'd get Range frames too.

Important: after updateBoundsIfNeeded, `RANGE 0 FOLLOWING` → `RANGE BETWEEN CURRENT ROW AND 0 FOLLOWING` → lowerBound is currentRow (isUnboundedOrCurrent true), upperBound is 0 FOLLOWING (offset bound) → offset RANGE → needs Range support → single key check etc.

Let me now write the summary.

Structure:
## Objective
Add native value-based RANGE window framing (WindowFrame.Range) to Druid's native window engine plus SQL-layer support, in /app, so single-key numeric/timestamp RANGE offset frames work end-to-end while unsupported forms (multi-key, non-numeric keys) keep failing.

## Important Details
- All the class/API constraints from prior summary (getters, factory, JsonSubTypes "range", not extending OffsetFrame, equals/hashCode, toString, serde).
- Frame resolution semantics: boundValue(v,o) = asc ? v+o : v-o; ASC frame = closed [lowerBound, upperBound] with frameStart = first row x >= lowerBound, frameEnd = first row x > upperBound; DESC frameStart = first row x <= lowerBound, frameEnd = first row x < upperBound; offsets signed (PRECEDING negative, FOLLOWING positive, CURRENT ROW = 0.0, UNBOUNDED = null).
- NULL handling: nulls sort first ASC / last DESC (compareRows null lhs = -1, × direction for DESC); nulls contiguous single peer group; null current row + finite bound → boundary of null run; unbounded → partition edge including nulls.
- Comparisons in double space via accessor.getDouble(); accepts >2^53 precision fuzz.
- Aggregation: outputRows = peer group, inputRows = frame interval; empty frames → existing AggIntervalCursor.moveTo → fresh aggregators → zero-row default; FIRST/LAST_VALUE empty frame → null.
- isEffectivelyUnbounded for Range = both offsets null → same as unbounded.
- Shared resolver class (e.g. RangeFrameResolver) in org.apache.druid.query.operator.window using ClusteredGroupPartitioner.fromRAC(rac).computeBoundaries(single orderBy column) for peer groups; both DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable and WindowValueProcessorBase use it.
- Only DefaultFramedOnHeapAggregatable implements FramedOnHeapAggregatable; concrete RACs don't override as() for it.
- SQL layer: Calcite 1.41 parser frame switch only ROWS (createBoolean(true)) / RANGE (createBoolean(false)); GROUPS not parseable → !SqlWindow.isRows() ⟺ RANGE; Window.Group Rel level only boolean isRows.
- Validator: keep message "Order By with RANGE clause currently supports only UNBOUNDED or CURRENT ROW. Use ROWS clause instead." for rejected cases; allow offset RANGE only with exactly one ORDER BY key (orderList.size()==1) and bound literals numeric or day-time INTERVAL (isValidEndpoint already requires SqlUtil.isLiteral); year-month intervals rejected; no ORDER BY → still rejected by count check.
- Planning (Windowing.getWindowFrame): keep groups path for unbounded/current-only bounds (preserves string-key behavior); Range only when ≥1 bound is PRECEDING/FOLLOWING offset; key type check via sig.getColumnType(fieldIndex) must be LONG/FLOAT/DOUBLE (TIMESTAMP maps to LONG via Calcites.isLongType/getColumnTypeForRelDataType).
- getBoundAsDouble: unbounded→null, currentRow→0.0, preceding→-value, following→+value, value = ((Number) window.constants.get(refIndex - sig.size()).getValue()).doubleValue(); day-time interval RexLiteral value = BigDecimal of millis (SqlParserUtil.intervalToMillis), year-month → months → reject.
- Quidem !error matches squash(stackTrace).contains(squash(expected)); expected text is just "QueryInterruptedException"; qaWin RANGE queries are multi-key or no-ORDER-BY (both keep failing) — full multiline verification of all files still pending.
- testUnSupportedRangeBounds (CalciteQueryTest.java ~15619) uses single STRING key + ROW_NUMBER — would now succeed (ROW_NUMBER ignores frame, string key check at planning never runs) → test must be updated.
- ROW_NUMBER/LAG/LEAD/rank fns don't consume the frame (getWindowFrame only called for FIRST_VALUE/LAST_VALUE and framed aggregations).
